## Page 1


BERNSTEIN–VON MISES THEOREM FOR SPARSE GENERALIZED
LINEAR MODELS
BY HANQING LIa
AND XUEWEN LUb
Department of Mathematics and Statistics, University of Calgary, ahanqing.li@ucalgary.ca, bxlu@ucalgary.ca
We study spike-and-slab priors for generalized linear models with possi-
ble grouped sparsity. The main result is an oracle Bernstein–von Mises the-
orem for the fractional posterior under supportwise likelihood assumptions.
The proof develops sparse local asymptotic normality and Laplace approxi-
mation around support-specific pseudo-true centers, and combines them with
fixed-prior mass, support penalization, recovery geometry, and beta-min sep-
aration to obtain contraction, support recovery, Gaussian mixture approxima-
tion, and collapse to the oracle Gaussian law. Model-entry verifications are
given for Gaussian regression and for logistic, Poisson, probit, Gamma log-
link, and negative-binomial log-link regression under stated sufficient condi-
tions. The ordinary posterior is treated only through restricted Gaussian and
canonical-link extensions, with coverage under additional active-dimension
and moment conditions.
1. Introduction.
The Bernstein–von Mises (BvM) theorem explains why, in regular
finite dimensional problems, Bayesian credible sets and frequentist confidence sets agree
asymptotically (Le Cam, 1986; van der Vaart, 1998). In sparse high-dimensional models the
same conclusion is harder because one must control local likelihood geometry and the poste-
rior distribution over a combinatorial support space.
Bayesian variable selection based on spike-and-slab (SAS) priors has a long history, begin-
ning with subset selection formulations such as Mitchell and Beauchamp (1988) and stochas-
tic search implementations such as George and McCulloch (1993). Modern high-dimensional
theory has clarified when sparse Bayesian procedures achieve contraction, selection consis-
tency, and uncertainty quantification in Gaussian linear models (Narisetty and He, 2014;
Castillo, Schmidt-Hieber and van der Vaart, 2015; Martin, Mess and Walker, 2017; Belitser
and Ghosal, 2020; Yang, Wainwright and Jordan, 2016). Related approaches include nonlocal
prior model selection (Johnson and Rossell, 2012) and the broader Bayesian variable selec-
tion literature reviewed in Tadesse and Vannucci (2021). For sparse generalized linear models
(GLM), earlier work studied many-covariate normal approximations (Ghosal, 1997), fitted
density convergence and subset modeling (Jiang, 2007; Liang, Song and Yu, 2013), posterior
contraction (Jeong and Ghosal, 2021), empirical Bayes BvM limits (Tang and Martin, 2024),
and sharp local likelihood/model selection analysis for data-dependent priors (Lee, Chae and
Martin, 2025). A fixed-prior exact support distributional theory remains less developed for
sparse GLM-type likelihoods.
This paper develops such a fixed-prior theory for SAS priors, allowing grouped sparsity.
The main distributional result approximates the fractional posterior by a Gaussian mixture
over sparse supersets of the true support and proves collapse of that mixture to the oracle
Gaussian law. The endpoint is stronger than selection consistency alone, because it identifies
the limiting posterior law after exact support recovery. The contraction part follows the clas-
sical testing, sieve, and prior mass strategy for posterior convergence (Ghosal, Ghosh and
MSC2020 subject classifications. Primary 62G20, 62F15; secondary 62J12.
Keywords and phrases. Bernstein–von Mises theorem, Generalized linear models, Spike-and-slab prior, High-
dimensional inference, Fractional posterior, Finite-sample likelihood geometry.

van der Vaart, 2000; Ghosal and van der Vaart, 2007), adapted here to supportwise sparse
likelihoods and fractional posteriors. The paper-specific steps are the supportwise sparse lo-
cal asymptotic normality (LAN) expansion around pseudo-true centers, the perturbation from
local Laplace support weights to quadratic score weights, and the strict superset projection
comparison combined with fixed-prior support penalization.
Section 8 serves as a model-entry layer. The abstract supportwise theory of Sections 2–
7 applies once the corresponding assumptions are verified. Gaussian regression is verified
directly. Nonlinear GLM examples are handled through a separate controlled-window frac-
tional route, where the analysis on sparse supersets of S0 is truth centered and underfitted
supports are removed by fractional Rényi separation. A conservative fixed-window canonical
bridge is retained only for the all-support pseudo-true verification and the restricted ordinary-
posterior add-on.
The paper makes the following contributions:
(C1) supportwise sparse LAN and uniform Laplace approximation for supportwise likeli-
hoods.
(C2) an exact-support posterior chain covering dimension control, contraction, underfitted
exclusion, strict-superset removal, and support recovery.
(C3) Gaussian-mixture approximation and collapse to the oracle Gaussian law.
(C4) model-entry verifications for Gaussian regression, controlled-window nonlinear GLM,
a conservative fixed-window canonical bridge, and the restricted ordinary-posterior plug-
in and coverage extension.

BVM THEOREM FOR SPARSE GLM
3
in the active groups indexed by S, and s = |S| is the number of active groups. Let ˜βS ∈Rp
denote the ambient zero padded embedding of βS, meaning that (˜βS)g = βg for g ∈S and
(˜βS)g = 0 for g /∈S. The group support of the ambient regression vector is Sβ = {g : βg ̸= 0}

ter vector, its support, and the true groupwise and coordinatewise cardinalities, respectively.
When mg = 1 for all g, the grouped formulation reduces to ordinary coordinatewise sparsity.
Grouped sparsity has an established frequentist literature, beginning with the group lasso
of Yuan and Lin (2006) and including optimal rate theory such as Huang and Zhang (2010);
Lounici et al. (2011). Grouped Bayesian procedures based on continuous spike-and-slab reg-
ularization include Bai (2025), Bai et al. (2022), Komodromos et al. (2025), and Ge, Lin
and Liu (2025). The present paper instead works with a Dirac SAS prior, so the support it-
self is a discrete posterior object and exact support recovery can be studied directly. In the
grouped setting this changes the inferential target. The prior is placed on active groups rather
than coordinates, the slab factorizes by groups, the beta-min condition is groupwise, and the
recovery statements use the group ℓ2,1 norm.

1.3. Organization of the paper.
Section 2 introduces the posterior formulation, the SAS
prior, and the assumptions. Section 3 develops the supportwise sparse LAN core, including
score control, localization, Laplace approximation, and an evidence lower bound. Sections 4,
5, and 6 then use that local analysis to prove contraction, exact support recovery, the Gaussian
mixture approximation, and the oracle BvM theorem for the fractional posterior. Section 7


## Page 4


4
gives the ordinary posterior extension, namely a plug-in Gaussian approximation and pos-
terior credibility result followed, under additional fixed active dimension, weighted active
block leverage, and third moment conditions, by a frequentist coverage theorem for the or-
dinary posterior. Section 8 gives model-entry verifications. Gaussian regression is directly
verified on the likelihood side, nonlinear GLM examples are reduced to controlled-window
Fisher, leverage, link-envelope, and beta-min conditions, and the conservative fixed-window
canonical bridge is kept for the auxiliary all-support verification. Section 9 concludes, and
the supplement contains the technical proofs.
2. Preliminaries.
This section fixes the posterior formulation, the grouped SAS prior,
and the assumption layers used throughout the paper. The assumptions are organized to mir-
ror the proof strategy. Design and dimensionality conditions control the combinatorial com-
plexity of the sparse model class. Supportwise likelihood conditions identify the local score
and curvature regime in which a quadratic approximation is valid. Prior conditions guarantee
enough local mass near the true sparse parameter and enough penalization of redundant sup-
ports. The final recovery and localization conditions turn that finite-sample likelihood control
into contraction, model selection, and the oracle Gaussian limit.
2.1. Fractional posterior.
In addition to the ordinary posterior, we consider the fractional
posterior, also known as the power, tempered, or α-posterior (Bhattacharya, Pati and Yang,
2019; Alquier and Ridgway, 2020). Fractional posteriors and related Gibbs/general posterior
constructions are useful both theoretically and practically (Syring and Martin, 2019; Mar-
tin and Syring, 2022). Tempering the likelihood often stabilizes high-dimensional posterior
analysis and leads to cleaner concentration arguments.
For α ∈(0,1], the fractional posterior is defined by

2.2. Spike-and-slab prior under grouped sparsity.
Castillo, Schmidt-Hieber and van der
Vaart (2015) show that independent Laplace priors need not provide Bayesian uncertainty
quantification matching the sparse contraction behavior of their posterior modes. Posterior


## Page 5


BVM THEOREM FOR SPARSE GLM
5
contraction and credible-set coverage may fail at the desired high-dimensional scale. Spike-
and-slab priors address this difficulty by placing explicit prior mass on sparse supports while
retaining continuous uncertainty within selected models. See Tadesse and Vannucci (2021)
for a comprehensive review.
In the grouped predictor setting, we adopt a classical SAS prior with a Dirac spike at zero.
The construction proceeds as follows:
Step 1. Draw the group cardinality. Sample the number of active groups s from a prior
distribution with mass function πG.
Step 2. Select the active set. Conditional on s, choose a support S ⊆{1,...,G} of size s
uniformly at random from the
 G
s

possible subsets.
Step 3. Draw the active coefficients. Conditional on S, draw βS from a slab density ϕS on
RpS, and set βSc = 0.
The resulting joint prior for (S,β) is
(5)
(S,β) 7→πG(s)
 G
s
 ϕS(βS)δ0(βSc),
where δ0 denotes a point mass at zero on Rp−pS. Thus the prior enforces exact sparsity by
assigning zero coefficients to all inactive groups. The support of β is then identified with
the selected active set S, which makes this formulation natural for studying model selection
consistency and exact support recovery.
The exact sparsity feature of (5) is central to the asymptotic analysis. Because inactive
groups are set to zero, posterior mass can be decomposed support by support rather than only
through approximate thresholding. This permits both the Gaussian mixture representation
over sparse models and the final oracle collapse onto the true support.
This grouped construction includes the ordinary coordinatewise SAS prior as a special
case when mg = 1 for all g = 1,...,G. For the contraction results in Section 4, the general
slab density ϕS is sufficient. For the later comparison of posterior weights over supersets
of the true support, we additionally impose a factorized slab structure, namely ϕS(βS) =
Q
g∈S ϕg(βg).
2.3. Regularity conditions.
The paper is written at this blockwise level to separate the
likelihood, prior, and recovery layers. Assumption 1 fixes the sparse dimensional regime.
Assumption 2 gives the supportwise score and local information inputs. Assumption 3 sup-
plies exact sparsity prior mass and support penalization. Assumption 4 converts prediction
control into coefficient recovery and imposes the beta-min scale needed for exact support
recovery. Assumption 5 is used only for the ordinary posterior extension.
Throughout, let ϵn =
p
s0 log G/n. This is the target rate in the paper. It represents the
effective cost of searching over s0 active groups among G candidates.
2.3.1. Sparse regime and design.
This block fixes the dimensional regime and the basic
design growth conditions used throughout the paper.
ASSUMPTION 1 (Sparse regime and design).
The following hold:
(i) For every fixed C > 0, xn(Cs0) ⪯√s0 log G.
(ii) mmax = max1≤g≤G mg ⪯log G/log n.
(iii) n,G →∞, 1 ≤s0, and s0 log G ≺n.
Assumption 1(i) is a sparse row condition. It controls rows restricted to supports of order
s0, not the full ambient row norm. Thus, in coordinate sparse high-dimensional designs, the

√

sparse row condition compatible with the sparse Fisher invertibility imposed later.
Assumption 1(ii) is a relative growth condition on the group sizes. It allows mmax to
grow whenever the ratio log G/log n grows, as can occur in super-polynomial or ultra-high-
dimensional regimes. In polynomial-dimensional regimes the bound specializes to mmax =
O(1). Thus growing groups are allowed, provided their size remains within the logarithmic
complexity scale available from the support search.

Assumption 2 supplies the likelihood side inputs of pseudo-true centering, moderate devia-
tion score control, local information stability, and the Hellinger prediction comparison used in
posterior contraction. Posterior dimension control, wrong support exclusion, and oracle col-
lapse require the prior and recovery blocks as well. The score condition is local in the window
|λ| ≤amgfgn, not a global sub Gaussian requirement. The curvature condition is the comple-
mentary uniform quadratic approximation on the ellipsoids of radius Rn(s) = Mn
√slog G.
Part (v) is stated for the observed information Fn,S(βS), not for the Fisher information alone.
Thus the abstract LAN layer does not assume that the observed Hessian equals its expecta-
tion. In noncanonical links the centered residual-Hessian term is part of the quantity that must
be controlled. Section 8 records how these inputs are verified or assumed for the models con-
sidered.

Assumption 3 is the exact sparsity prior analogue of the usual small ball and complexity
prior requirements in high-dimensional Bayes theory. Part (i) now separates the direct exact
support prior mass needed in the evidence lower bound from the recursive support size tail
control used later in posterior dimension arguments. Part (ii) supplies the companion local
mass around the true active coefficients. Part (iii) imposes local flatness on the active slab, and
part (iv) gives the extra relative support penalty needed later to suppress redundant supersets.
In Section 8, the complexity/Beta–Binomial support priors and the Gaussian/Laplace group
slabs verify these requirements directly.

Here a9 is the sparse invertibility constant, a10 is the localization smallness constant, and
a11 is the group beta-min constant. The normalization a9 ≤1 is without loss of generality.
If a larger lower bound is available, replace it by min(a9,1). The first two parts of Assump-
tion 4 convert prediction control into coefficient control and ensure that the Hellinger scale
contraction from Section 4 stays inside the quadratic region used by the sparse LAN layer.
The third part is the group beta-min requirement. Its order, ϵn/φ2(s), is the sufficient support
recovery scale used by the present contraction argument, and later the exact support theo-
rem only requires the signal constant a11 to dominate the explicit recovery constant from
Theorem 6. The numerical threshold is conservative and is not claimed to be selection opti-
mal. In the contraction theorem the same localization smallness condition is also used to turn
Hellinger control into prediction control. The theorem statement below makes the required
smallness explicit through the inequality a10Kpred(α) ≤1, so the proof does not rely on an
implicit strengthening of Assumption 4(ii).
REMARK 2.
The beta-min scale in Assumption 4(iii) is a sufficient recovery condition
for the theorem chain below. It is driven by the conversion from global contraction to coef-
ficient recovery rather than by a support-specific Neyman–Pearson or likelihood separation
analysis. We do not claim that this threshold is selection optimal.
2.3.5. Ordinary posterior predictor sieve.
This final assumption is needed only when
α = 1. It is the same predictor envelope sieve throughout the ordinary posterior branch, with
model dependence entering only through the envelope level Ln and whether the sieve can be
verified under the chosen slab.

For the ordinary posterior there

Assumption 5 is a prior side tail bound, not a posterior conclusion. It is separate from
the local slab mass and flatness requirements in Assumption 3. For the Gaussian and group-
Laplace slabs used in the examples, the supplement verifies this sieve by explicit tail bounds
involving the sparse row envelope xn(Cs0) and the envelope level Ln. In the ordinary
posterior arguments the sieve is combined with the evidence lower bound, so we write
a∗

12 > 0. The fractional posterior theorem chain does not use
this assumption.
The theorem chain proceeds as follows. Section 3 establishes the supportwise likelihood
core, including score control, local quadratic bracketing, Laplace approximation, an evidence
lower bound, and the strict-superset comparison. Sections 4 and 5 combine this likelihood
layer with the prior and recovery blocks to obtain dimension control, contraction, exclu-
sion of underfitted supports, removal of strict supersets, and exact support recovery. Sec-
tion 6 turns support recovery and local Gaussian approximations into the Gaussian-mixture
approximation and oracle Gaussian collapse. Section 7 gives the ordinary-posterior plug-in
and coverage extensions, while Section 8 records the model-entry verifications and auxiliary
conditions.

6. Distributional approximation.
This section contains the final distributional conse-
quences of the paper. It turns the earlier likelihood core together with the posterior selection
theorems into a global distributional approximation and, for the fractional posterior, gives the
cleanest BvM conclusions. The first result identifies the posterior with a Gaussian mixture
over sparse supersets of the true support. The second shows that, once wrong supports are
removed, this mixture collapses to the oracle Gaussian law indexed by S0.

8. Examples.
This section records model-entry verifications for familiar sparse GLM
settings. It does not replace the abstract theorem chain in Sections 2–7. It identifies the like-
lihood, prior, recovery, and sieve inputs used in each model class.
8.1. Verification roadmap.
There are three entries. Gaussian regression verifies the ab-
stract likelihood block directly. The nonlinear GLM route below gives a self-contained frac-
tional posterior result under fixed-design controlled-window conditions. The conservative
fixed-window canonical bridge is retained only for the all-support pseudo-true verification
used by the restricted ordinary-posterior add-on.
The nonlinear fractional route is not a verification of Assumption 2 on all sparse supports.
It uses truth-Fisher LAN and Laplace approximation on sparse supersets of S0, while un-
derfitted supports are excluded by fractional Rényi separation. Thus it proves Proposition 2
directly and does not imply ordinary posterior sieve control. That control enters only through
Assumption 5 in the restricted α = 1 branch.
The model-entry conditions below are stated through sparse Fisher eigenvalues, sparse in-
fluence and leverage quantities, derivative envelopes, beta-min separation, and prior admis-
sibility. The detailed likelihood reductions, prior calculations, and sieve checks are deferred
to the supplement.


## Page 15


BVM THEOREM FOR SPARSE GLM
15
8.2. Prior examples.
The prior side uses standard Dirac SAS choices. The support size
prior may be either the complexity prior πG(s) ∝c−sG−As, with fixed c > 0 and A large
enough, or the Beta–Binomial prior induced by
γg | θ

groups and is either group Gaussian, ϕg = Nmg(0,σ2Img), or group Laplace, ϕg(βg) =
cmg,λe−λ∥βg∥2 with fixed λ > 0.
The supplementary prior calculations give the true-support mass, recursive support tail,
strict-superset penalty, coefficient small-ball bound, slab flatness on Fisher ellipsoids, and
sparse global envelope needed in Assumption 3 and in (U3) below. For α = 1, the supplement
also gives the predictor-envelope sieve under separate slab-tail conditions involving Ln and
the sparse row envelope. Below, “one of the prior examples” means one of these support
priors and slabs with the associated supplementary scale conditions.

Ordinary posterior extension.
For α = 1, the ordinary posterior extension is not part of the
unified fractional theorem. It is stated only for Gaussian linear regression and, conditionally,
for canonical logistic or Poisson regression through the conservative fixed-window canonical
bridge. In either case, the ordinary posterior additionally requires Assumption 5, the entropy
condition and sieve exponent gap in Theorem 6, and the downstream support-recovery and
plug-in conditions stated in Sections 4–7.

The proof is separate from Theorem 7. It uses its own evidence lower bound, fractional

PROPOSITION 3 (Conservative fixed-window canonical bridge).
For canonical logistic
or Poisson regression, the high-level supportwise likelihood Assumption 2 holds if the conser-
vative fixed-window canonical bridge conditions stated in the supplement hold. This device
is used only for the ordinary posterior extension and predictor-window diagnostics, not for
the unified fractional GLM theorem.
COROLLARY 3 (Gaussian and nonlinear GLM summary).
Under the common baseline
prior, recovery, support-budget, and support-penalty conditions, the Gaussian likelihood ver-
ification in the supplement gives the fractional posterior conclusions of Sections 4–6. For the
five nonlinear GLM models listed above, Proposition 1 and Proposition 2 give the fractional
exact-support and oracle BvM conclusions under the controlled-window conditions.
COROLLARY 4 (Projection-stable fixed-window canonical bridge).
In fixed-window lo-
gistic or Poisson regression, deterministic sparse weighted projection stability (WPS), the
bounded truth-envelope condition, the sparse Gram and score-window conditions, and the
local-information smallness condition imply the conservative fixed-window canonical bridge.
Hence the ordinary posterior extension is nonempty under explicit design conditions.
COROLLARY 5 (Probit verification).
In probit regression, if the truth-Fisher weights dis-
played above and the controlled-window conditions (V1), (V2), (V3a), (V3b), and (V4)–(V6)
of Proposition 1 hold, then the fractional conclusions of Proposition 2 hold.

COROLLARY 8 (Ordinary posterior extension).
For α = 1, the ordinary posterior exten-
sion stated here is restricted to Gaussian linear regression and canonical logistic or Poisson
regression through Proposition 3. In those branches, if the common predictor-envelope sieve
from Assumption 5, the bounded predictor-window entropy condition from Theorem 6, and
the downstream side conditions through Theorem 11 hold, then the plug-in conclusions of
Theorem 12 and Corollary 2 apply. Under the additional fixed active dimension and active-
block moment conditions from Theorem 13, the frequentist coverage conclusion also applies.
The noncanonical sieve calculations in the supplement are recorded only as prior-side
supporting estimates. They do not by themselves yield an ordinary-posterior noncanonical
BvM, because the α = 1 argument would also require ordinary-posterior testing/localization
and plug-in observed-information control. We do not state that extension here.


## Page 20


20
9. Discussion.
We have established fixed-prior exact support BvM limits for sparse sup-
portwise likelihoods motivated by GLM. The contribution has two parts. The first is a sup-
portwise likelihood core that supplies pseudo-true centering, score control, information sta-
bility, and Laplace approximation on sparse supports. The second combines that core with
exact sparsity prior mass, support penalization, recovery geometry, and beta-min separation
to obtain contraction, exclusion of wrong supports, Gaussian mixture approximation, and
final oracle collapse. For the nonlinear GLM examples treated in Section 8, we also give
a unified truth-Fisher fractional theorem that uses local LAN on sparse supersets and frac-
tional Rényi separation for underfitted supports, rather than pseudo-true LAN on all sparse
supports. The controlled-window proposition reduces these requirements to sparse Fisher,
leverage, derivative-envelope, and beta-min assumptions. This exact support distributional
limit is stronger than selection consistency alone, because it identifies the limiting posterior
law after the support has collapsed.
Relative to the current literature, our contribution is complementary to the empirical prior
approach of Lee, Chae and Martin (2025) and to empirical Bayes sparse GLM BvM theory
such as Tang and Martin (2024). Those approaches use data-dependent centering or scaling
to align the prior with local likelihood geometry, whereas the prior here is fixed before seeing
the data. The resulting assumptions involve likelihood regularity, recovery geometry, Rényi
or beta-min separation, and prior support penalties. Under these assumptions, fixed Dirac
SAS priors lead from supportwise local likelihood analysis through strict superset projec-
tion comparison and wrong-support elimination to Gaussian mixture and exact support BvM
conclusions. The nonlinear GLM verification separates the superset LAN problem from un-
derfitted fractional separation, which is different from proving selection consistency alone.
The result is also distinct from earlier sparse GLM Bayesian contraction and fitted density
results (Jiang, 2007; Jeong and Ghosal, 2021) and from classical or minimax model selection
analyses for GLM (Abramovich and Grinshtein, 2016; Rigollet, 2012). Those works clarify
important rate and selection phenomena, but they do not provide the fixed-prior distributional
support collapse statement pursued here.
The strongest assumptions have specific roles, but they are sufficient conditions rather than
optimality claims. In the abstract theorem chain, the beta-min condition is tied to exact sup-
port recovery rather than to contraction alone, and it is driven by the conversion from global
contraction to coefficient recovery. In the unified fractional GLM theorem, the analogous
signal requirement enters through the Rényi gap summability. The truth-Fisher sparse lower
bound keeps the oracle Gaussian covariance nondegenerate even when individual curvatures
degenerate along a growing predictor window. If truth-centered sparse Fisher constants decay,
then the contraction rate, score window scale, beta-min threshold, and Gaussian remainder
terms have to be rescaled simultaneously.
Several issues remain open. On the ordinary posterior side, one may seek the common
predictor envelope sieve from Assumption 5 in bounded-envelope logistic and probit models
without the extra sparse row decay used in the supplementary sufficient conditions. Sharper
sufficient conditions for the truth-Fisher influence, residual-Hessian, and Rényi steps would
make the nonlinear examples less conservative. Another direction is to allow decaying truth-
centered sparse Fisher constants by developing a rescaled theorem rather than imposing the
present nondegenerate recovery baseline. Finally, the support-specific pseudo-true centers
used in the abstract theorem chain address misspecification caused by restricting the likeli-
hood to an incorrect support. Global GLM misspecification would require a different target
and a corresponding redevelopment of the support recovery and Gaussian limit statements.
A separate direction is to develop an analogous distributional theory for continuous SAS
and related shrinkage priors, including global local shrinkage (Carvalho, Polson and Scott,
2010), the SAS lasso and continuous spike formulations (Roˇcková, 2018; Roˇcková and


## Page 21


BVM THEOREM FOR SPARSE GLM
21
George, 2018; Nie and Roˇcková, 2023), and grouped or variational continuous spike meth-
ods (Bai, 2025; Bai et al., 2022; Komodromos et al., 2025; Ge, Lin and Liu, 2025). Be-
cause a continuous spike typically does not produce literal exact sparsity, such a BvM theory
would likely need to be formulated through latent inclusion indicators, thresholded supports,
selected representatives, or other asymptotically sparse summaries. Developing that theory
while retaining meaningful uncertainty quantification remains an interesting open problem.

APPENDIX A: PROOFS FOR SECTION 3
The arguments in this section follow the same order as the likelihood layer in the main
text. The local bracketing viewpoint is in the finite-sample local asymptotic normality (LAN)
tradition of Spokoiny (2012, 2017), but the statements below are made uniformly over sparse
support classes and supportwise pseudo-true centers. We first prove the uniform score bound.
We then combine it with local information stability to localize the restricted maximizers, and
finally use those two ingredients in the Laplace approximation, the denominator lower bound,
and the supportwise projection estimate.
Throughout the supportwise likelihood layer, the restricted log likelihoods ℓn,S are un-
derstood to be twice continuously differentiable on the local ellipsoids under consideration
and jointly measurable in (Y,βS). Hence compact-ellipsoid maximizers may be chosen by a
measurable selection, and the integral Taylor expansions below are well defined.

APPENDIX B: PROOFS FOR SECTION 4
The proofs in this section use the ingredients from Section 3 in order. The testing, sieve,
and prior mass backbone is the standard posterior contraction device of Ghosal, Ghosh and
van der Vaart (2000); Ghosal and van der Vaart (2007). Here it is applied after the supportwise
likelihood layer has reduced the problem to sparse predictor shells. Lemma 1 provides the
denominator-good event, Theorem 5 removes overly large supports, Lemma B.1 isolates the
Hellinger/prediction comparison used in both branches of Theorem 6, Lemma B.2 supplies
the bounded window shell test needed only in the ordinary posterior branch, and Theorems 6
and 7 then turn those ingredients into contraction and underfitting exclusion. The fractional
branch of Theorem 6 is handled separately by a direct Hellinger transform bound, so no
global covering of the full sparse parameter space is used there.

±,n.
For Gaussian regression, the constants in Lemma B.1 are furnished by the direct Gaus-
sian verification. For logistic and Poisson regression under a bounded predictor envelope,
they are furnished by the canonical bounded-window calculations once the truth and pseudo-
true predictors are inside the envelope. Under a diverging predictor envelope, the pairwise
bounded-window upper comparison is available on the predictor sieve, but the global truth-
side truncated lower comparison in part (iii) is not automatic. The unified fractional GLM
theorem in Section 8 avoids this global comparison by using truth-centered local likelihood
control on supersets and fractional Rényi separation on underfitted supports.

exponents match the denominator bound from Lemma 1. The fractional branch is handled
separately and does not use this lemma.

APPENDIX D: PROOFS FOR SECTION 6
This section proves the distributional approximation layer. The first auxiliary lemmas com-
pare the exact supportwise posterior kernels with their Gaussian proxies, and the final ar-
guments assemble those local comparisons into the Gaussian mixture and oracle-collapse
statements from the main text.
Before proving the Gaussian mixture approximation we isolate the four ingredients that
enter the argument. The first lemma compares the exact supportwise posterior kernel with the
Gaussian kernel centered at the localized maximizer. The second lemma turns that compari-
son into a conditional Gaussian approximation on each support. The third lemma compares
the localized support weights with the quadratic score weights used in the main theorem. The
fourth lemma then assembles the localized posterior from these supportwise pieces. Through-
out this section we write
S†
n = {S ⊆{1,...,G} : S ⊇S0, |S| ≤Kdims0},
for the post dimension control sparse superset class from the main text, keeping it distinct
from the generic size budget classes Sn(s) and S+

APPENDIX E: PROOFS FOR SECTION 7
The proofs in this section mirror the layered plug-in and ordinary posterior extensions
in the main text. The first two results concern the plug-in refinement that is valid for every
α ∈(0,1]. We first replace the oracle Gaussian law by its plug-in version and then transfer
the posterior credibility statement to the plug-in credible set. Only the final theorem is the
genuinely ordinary posterior extension, and for that reason the two auxiliary lemmas proved
at the end of the section are reserved for the frequentist-coverage step under α = 1.

APPENDIX F: PROOFS FOR SECTION 8.1
The proofs in Sections F–I refine the earlier score, curvature, and Hellinger calculations
into the model-entry format used in Section 8. Sections F–I contain four model-entry com-
ponents. First, Gaussian regression verifies the abstract likelihood block directly. Second, the
fixed-design controlled-window nonlinear GLM route verifies (U1)–(U5) and proves Propo-
sition 2 for fixed α ∈(0,1). Third, the conservative fixed-window canonical bridge veri-
fies the all-support pseudo-true likelihood block for logistic and Poisson regression under
stronger conditions. Fourth, the prior and predictor-sieve calculations verify the support and
slab requirements used by the main theorem chain.
The fixed-design controlled-window nonlinear GLM argument is self-contained. Propo-
sition 1 verifies (U1)–(U5), including the projected-score and residual-Hessian entropy in-
puts, and Proposition 2 proves its own dimension control, underfitted Rényi exclusion, strict-
superset removal, exact support recovery, and oracle BvM. This argument does not rely on
Theorem 7 or the later abstract BvM theorems unless Assumption 2 has been separately
verified.
The prior-side admissibility calculations invoked in Section 8.2 are collected separately in
Section G. The slab small-ball checks are prior-side calculations, but slab flatness on Fisher
ellipsoids also uses the Fisher-to-Euclidean conversion from the recovery geometry.
The nonlinear verification proposition is a self-contained fractional argument, with canon-
ical and noncanonical cases proved separately below. Proposition 3 is retained as the con-
servative fixed-window canonical bridge for canonical links, with Lemma F.2 explaining the
weighted projection localization mechanism. After these likelihood checks, the posterior con-
clusions in the main text still require the stated prior, support penalty, truth-Fisher, and Rényi
side conditions.
Concrete feasible regimes..
The conditions above are nonempty in standard sparse designs.
The following sufficient regimes are not used as additional assumptions. They show the scal-
ing requirements. Suppose, for example, that mmax = O(1), log G ≍log n, xn(s) ⪯√s uni-
formly for s ≍s0, and the relevant sparse Gram or sparse Fisher eigenvalues are bounded
above and below on supports of size O(s0). This sparse row condition is compatible
with ambient full row norms of order

The point of Lemma F.2 is to isolate the canonical representation step. In the logistic and
Poisson models, the pseudo-true predictor is always a weighted projection of the truth, but
the relevant weight matrix is the proof generated object W⋆
S. Proposition 3 therefore keeps
only the resulting pseudo-true predictor localization as the high-level verification input, rather
than formulating the verification in terms of a free-standing weighted projection assumption.
The next proposition shows that, on a bounded predictor window, this localization can in fact
be recovered from the deterministic sparse weighted projection stability condition (WPS) in
the main text.

The condition supS,W ∥PS,W∥∞→∞≤KΠ is kept separate from the sparse Gram bounds.
Sparse Gram bounds control Euclidean conditioning, while weighted projection stability con-
trols coordinatewise amplification after arbitrary positive diagonal reweighting. Without fur-
ther restrictions on the design or the weights, sparse eigenvalue bounds alone do not control
this sup norm operator. The following proposition records a more restrictive alternative based
on small sparse row norms or sparse leverage. It is a supplementary sufficient condition. The
fixed-window canonical bridge in the main text uses the projection-stability argument above.

PROPOSITION F.3 (Primitive Gaussian verification).
For Gaussian linear regression with
τ−≤τi ≤τ+, predictor envelope ∥Xβ0∥∞≤Ln/2, and log Ln ⪯log n, Assumption 2(i)–

PROPOSITION F.4 (Gaussian reduction to the common contraction and recovery theo-
rems).
Assume the conditions of Proposition F.3, Assumptions 3, 4, and 6, and suppose
that the smallness constant in Assumption 4(ii) satisfies a10Kpred ≤1. If α = 1, also assume
the ordinary posterior side conditions from Theorem 6. Then Theorem 6 applies in Gaussian
linear regression. If, in addition, the signal constant in Assumption 4(iii) satisfies a11 > Krec,
then Theorem 7 applies as well.
PROOF OF PROPOSITION F.4. By Proposition F.3, Assumption 2 holds for Gaussian lin-
ear regression. The remaining assumptions in the proposition are those required to invoke
Theorem 6, including the common recovery-geometry smallness condition a10Kpred ≤1
and, when α = 1, the ordinary posterior side conditions. Hence Theorem 6 applies. If in ad-
dition a11 > Krec, then the extra signal condition needed for Theorem 7 is also satisfied, so
that theorem applies as well.
Fixed-window canonical Hellinger comparison..
Fix L < ∞and consider either Bernoulli
logistic regression or Poisson regression with canonical log link. There are constants 0 <
cL ≤CL < ∞such that, for all |η0| ≤L/2 and all η ∈R,
h2(fη,fη0) ≥cL{(η −η0)2 ∧1},
and, for all |η| ∨|η′| ≤L,
h2(fη,fη′) ≤CL(η −η′)2.
The truth-centered lower comparison is the fixed-window form of the one-observation GLM
Hellinger comparison used, for example, in Lemma A1 of Jeong and Ghosal (2021). Since
the canonical curvature at η0 is bounded above and below on [−L/2,L/2], the first dis-
play is equivalent, after changing cL, to the truth-side lower bound in Assumption 2(vi).
Indeed, for each of the two one observation canonical families, the map (η,η′) 7→h2(fη,fη′)
is continuous. Its second order Taylor expansion along the diagonal has positive coeffi-
cient proportional to the Fisher curvature. Hence, uniformly over |η0| ≤L/2, the ratio
h2(fη,fη0)/(η −η0)2 is bounded below by a positive constant for |η −η0| small. On the
compact set {|η0| ≤L/2, |η −η0| ≥ε, |η| ≤M} the same ratio with (η −η0)2 ∧1 in the
denominator has a positive minimum. Letting M →∞, the Hellinger distance has a positive
limiting lower bound in both logistic and Poisson regression when |η0| ≤L/2 and |η| →∞.
This proves the first display. The second display follows from the bounded derivative of
η 7→
p
fη in L2 on the compact interval [−L,L].

APPENDIX H: PROOFS FOR SECTION 8.3
The proof of Proposition 2 is self-contained and is not an application of Theorems 7, 10, or
11 unless the high-level Assumption 2 has been separately verified. On sparse supersets of S0
it uses truth-Fisher LAN, truth-centered Laplace approximation, projected-score comparison,
and radial localization. Underfitted supports are excluded by fractional Rényi separation.
Thus the nonlinear fractional route proves exact support recovery and oracle BvM directly
for fixed α ∈(0,1), and it does not by itself provide the ordinary-posterior extension.
Canonical case under predictor envelopes.
This part proves the canonical case of Propo-
sition 2. The argument is truth-Fisher based on sparse supersets of S0. The LAN, Laplace,
and tail-localization steps use only truth-Fisher quantities. Underfitted supports are different.
For the fractional posterior, they are excluded by the exact Rényi transform rather than by
restricted maximum likelihood estimators or an underfitted empirical-process theorem.

It remains to prove a global truncated lower bound when |η0| ≤C. On |u| ≤1 this follows

Jα(η0 + u,η0)
u2/(1 + u2)
has a positive minimum because the numerator is zero only at u = 0. Letting M →∞, the
same positive separation persists in logistic regression since a Bernoulli law with η0 in a com-
pact interior window has positive Rényi divergence from the limiting point masses reached
as u →±∞. In Poisson regression, the candidate mean tends either to 0 or to ∞as u →−∞
or u →∞. The Rényi divergence from a compact truth mean is respectively bounded away
from zero or divergent. Combining the compact and tail regions proves the first display.

PROOF OF PROPOSITION 3. The proof checks the four bridge inputs in order. Pseudo-
true localization comes from the predictor-window condition, Fisher block comparison from
sparse Gram and curvature bounds, score and local information from the canonical transfer
lemmas, and the Hellinger comparison from the stated window condition. Under the sparse
Gram lower bound from (F2), Lemma F.1 gives for every S ∈Sn(¯sn) a unique unrestricted
pseudo-true center β◦

PROOF OF COROLLARY 3. In the Gaussian case, Proposition F.3 verifies Assumption 2
for Gaussian linear regression. Once the common baseline conditions are combined with
Assumptions 3, 4, and 6, Proposition F.4 reduces this case to the contraction and underfitting
theorems used elsewhere in the paper, including the common recovery-geometry smallness
condition a10Kpred ≤1. The proofs of Theorems 8, 9, 10, and 11 therefore apply to the
Gaussian case through the same theorem chain, with direct verification at the likelihood layer.
For each nonlinear GLM under the fixed-design controlled-window conditions, Propo-
sition 1 verifies (U1)–(U5), and Proposition 2 supplies the stated exact-support and total
variation conclusions. If the conservative fixed-window canonical bridge is used for logis-
tic or Poisson regression, the displayed model inventories invoke the high-level verification
assumptions from Proposition 3, namely the bounded truth envelope, pseudo-true predictor
localization, sparse Gram control, the truth-to-pseudo score ratio, the resulting score window
lower bound, and the associated localization smallness bound. Lemma F.2 gives the weighted
projection representation behind that localization input.
On the prior side, Propositions G.1, G.2, and G.3 verify Assumption 3. Under the down-
stream side conditions stated in the corollary for the Gaussian and fixed-window canon-
ical bridge cases, namely a10Kpred(α) ≤1, a11 > Krec(α), and a8 −1 −Kpr > 0 with
Ksup ∈(0,a8 −1 −Kpr), the displayed fractional posterior conclusions follow with the
scope split in the main text. The Gaussian case uses direct Gaussian likelihood verification
plus the downstream theorem chain, the nonlinear GLM cases use Propositions 1 and 2, and
the canonical bridge case uses the conservative fixed-window canonical bridge.

Thus all conditions (F1)–(F4) of Proposition 3 hold with Ln ≡L, so the conservative

a∗
12 > 0, together with the downstream support-recovery, strict-superset, and plug-in side
conditions stated in Corollary 8, the ordinary-posterior extension is available in this fixed-
window canonical regime. In particular, Theorem 12 and Corollary 2 apply, and Theorem 13
applies under its additional fixed active-dimension and active-block moment assumptions.
The bridge alone supplies the likelihood-side entry for this extension and does not by itself
imply the ordinary posterior conclusion.
PROOF OF COROLLARIES 5, 6, AND 7. Lemma H.12 gives the model-specific likelihood
formulas, truth-Fisher weights, and log-concavity for probit, Gamma log link, and negative
binomial log link regression. Under the fixed-design controlled-window conditions stated in
the corollaries, Proposition 1 verifies (U1)–(U5). Therefore Proposition 2 gives exact support
recovery and the oracle Gaussian total-variation limit for the chosen noncanonical model.
The ordinary posterior sieve calculations are supporting results rather than theorem-level
consequences. Propositions G.4 and G.5 record the Gamma log and negative binomial log
link sieve verifications under the displayed slab tail conditions, and Proposition G.6 records
the bounded-envelope probit small sparse row variant as a separate side calculation.
PROOF OF COROLLARY 8. This corollary combines two ordinary-posterior cases. In the
Gaussian case, Proposition F.3 gives the likelihood verification, and Proposition F.4 reduces
the ordinary posterior argument to the common contraction and underfitting theorems used
elsewhere in the paper. In the canonical nonlinear case, Proposition 3 supplies the likelihood
block through the conservative fixed-window canonical bridge. In both cases the prior block
is furnished by Propositions G.1, G.2, and G.3.
After adding Assumption 5, the bounded predictor-window entropy condition from Theo-
rem 6, and the sieve exponent gap a∗
12 > 0, the ordinary posterior theorem chain through
Theorems 6–12 applies in the two cases listed in the corollary, under the stated down-
stream side conditions a10Kpred(1) ≤1, a11 > Krec(1), and a8 −1 −Kpr > 0 with Ksup ∈
(0,a8 −1 −Kpr). The Gaussian case uses Proposition F.4, and the canonical nonlinear case
uses Proposition 3. The conclusions of Theorem 12 and Corollary 2 therefore apply across
those two likelihood-side cases. Under the additional fixed active dimension and active block
conditions from Theorem 13, that theorem yields the coverage conclusion as well.
